     ```latex
     \paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
     The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
     
     \paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
     While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$) all leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\sim}$42\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.
     
     \paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
     [compressed version with m7 addition about effective reasoning in non-fatal steps]
     
     \paragraph{Practitioner implications.}
     [merged practitioner framework + pure-strategy gap, compressed]
     
     \paragraph{Framework applicability.}
     [compressed with MiniHack/Crafter boundary conditions]
     ```

     We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. Applied to ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; four intervention axes confirm robustness. Cross-domain probes validate predictions: no signal in MATH, information asymmetry in ALFWorld. Future work should target intermediate death regimes (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}) and stronger backbones.
     ```

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than death-prone tasks---non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. Counterfactual labels also derive from pure-strategy episodes; the 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with MATH), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",
        "numLines": 18,

Future work should target intermediate death regimes (NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}) and stronger backbones.\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "a67f9d66-6244-403c-9182-470140b9d160",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types. Since $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ with $P$ constant, $r \\to 1$ as DAF $\\to 1$; the near-perfect correlation is a structural consequence of death dominance, not an independent finding (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOf 95 non-tie routing labels across 245 episodes, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.\n\nUnder ScienceWorld's penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the full score gap is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $70.8\\%$ at $P{=}25$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-independent characterization. Given $\\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \\approx 90.3\\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are known.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels, while \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores) produces 0\\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\\% and DAF to 96.8\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF with Cohen's $d = 3.02$, indicating a large effect size; leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (DAF range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}). An independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). As a conservative sensitivity analysis, cluster-robust inference treating the $K{=}11$ task types as clusters ($p < 0.001$) confirms significance even under within-cluster correlation; 28-task directional consistency (100\\%) provides further support (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:per-task-ci}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $11.4\\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.\nBoth models exhibit nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%: Qwen $42.9\\%$, Llama $41.4\\%$), confirming that death-prone failure is structurally determined; the differential magnitude varies ($+26.5$~pp Qwen, $+30.0$~pp Llama) because deterministic strategy safety is model-dependent ($16.3\\%$ vs.\\ $11.4\\%$).\n\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1} shows cross-model precision matching ($55\\%$/$10\\%$/$+45$~pp in both models).\n\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} shows zero deaths in both models, consistent with its majority-class answer distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\\times}$ scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks (72B: independently collected at both internal and deterministic). Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\\% death at all scales). All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters; both internal and deterministic strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\\%$ death rate ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (7B: $90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance intact.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp over five trials). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $z = 2.75$, $p = 0.003$) then plateaus ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); the minimum detectable effect at $n{=}200$ is ${\\sim}14$~pp ($\\alpha = 0.05$, 80\\% power), indicating adequate sensitivity for the observed $N{=}1 \\to 3$ reduction but not for smaller plateau-region changes. Retry's per-trial conditional death rate stays constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$; Reflexion's declines to ${\\sim}31\\%$ by Trial~3 (per-task breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate as a function of trial count ($N$) for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Retry (blue) is nearly flat; Reflexion (red) drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus. Horizontal lines: 72B scaling baseline ($30.6\\%$, all 11 tasks) and deterministic baseline ($16.3\\%$). Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe evaluate two additional environments as boundary probes (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env-details}). ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different computation modes per environment: ScienceWorld uses an oracle simulator with ground-truth state, MATH uses a Python code interpreter, and ALFWorld uses admissible-action enumeration. These are not equivalent; results probe framework applicability rather than replicate findings.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter as deterministic computation mode), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp), with balanced labels (107 vs.\\ 95, 74.6\\% no-preference). Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is $27.4\\%$ vs.\\ $3.0\\%$ (35 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred, 98 no-preference). Of 37 non-tie labels, 35 (94.6\\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy, a one-directional dominance analogous to ScienceWorld's death dominance but driven by information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, 97.4\\% of comparisons are ties and only 0.3\\% of training steps conflict directionally with episode labels, leaving minimal step-level signal for the router to exploit. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).\n",
        "numLines": 113,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 113
      }
    },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "8e28ef87-1f56-42a6-bcf2-4ffe83334a69",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) and only 0.3\\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels. I tested this on a couple of other environments—MiniHack has no death mechanism at all, so there's no routing signal to exploit, while Crafter kills the agent regardless of strategy, meaning there's no asymmetry to leverage.     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than death-prone tasks---non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. Counterfactual labels also derive from pure-strategy episodes; the 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with MATH), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",

The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. Counterfactual labels also derive from pure-strategy episodes; the 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with MATH), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. ```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.
6	\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.
7	\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.
8	\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.
9	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
10	Recent concurrent work sharpens this question: \citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
13	CogRouter \cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.
14	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.
15	These optimize \emph{how} to route; we reveal \emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.
16	
17	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}
18	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.
19	Our approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \emph{before} any router is trained.
20	
21	\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}
22	ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.
23	
24	\paragraph{Safe RL.}
25	Safe RL methods \citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely.
26	
```

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\n\\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.\n\\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.\n\\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \\cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.\n\\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely.\n",

Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.",
          " ",

    "oldString": "Safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.",
    "newString": "Safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\n\\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.\n\\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.\n\\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \\cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.\n\\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 22,
        "oldLines": 4,

          " ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.",

Counterfactual labels also derive from pure-strategy episodes; the 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.",
    "newString": "Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. Counterfactual labels also derive from pure-strategy episodes; the 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal (consistent with MATH); universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",

The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RnGGCatG3uwB3hEZW977P7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$) all leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\sim}$42\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\sim}5\%$ \texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\% \to 29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}
14	A three-stage diagnostic (${\sim}$50-episode pilot $\to$ death-rate differential $\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Framework applicability.}
17	The penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\citep{minihack-2021} (0\% death) and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022} (100\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal (consistent with MATH); universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.
18	
```

</details>

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "oldString": "death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy",
    "newString": "death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal (consistent with MATH); universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 7,

The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification rather than generalizable features; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).",
          "+The router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).",

Research Reviewer 审查发现 "~27.7% mislabeling" 定义模糊且不可追溯。需要替换为两个精确指标：

"Of 2,040 training steps, only 0.3% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label); the remaining apparent mislabeling consists of episode labels applied to steps where neither strategy has a step-level advantage (60.3% pre-event neutral)."

**§2 scope 段落**（如果引用了 27.7%）也同步更新。

核心论点：mislabeling 的本质是 "applying a label to uninformative steps"（方向正确但粒度不匹配），不是 "applying a wrong label"（方向错误）。0.3% strict conflict 是为 episode-level 方法辩护的最强证据。

```text
107	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, 97.4\% of comparisons are ties and only 0.3\% of training steps conflict directionally with episode labels, leaving minimal step-level signal for the router to exploit. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.
111	
112	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
113	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01P9D3cpMSwGBjYT2b5qKb6P`</summary>

```text
14	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% of ScienceWorld steps produce identical scores regardless of computation mode; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes. The framework applies to any environment with controllable computation mode comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes.
18	
19	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
20	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic computation mode) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
21	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (11 task types, $N{=}220$) shows death dominance replicates across model families with direction consistency 10/10 ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ $+26.5$~pp for Qwen; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
```
