<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SnmzGpjnM9pxgUGRgWe1rG`</summary>

```text
35	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct cross-model replication (11 task types, $N{=}220$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}) shows death dominance replicates with direction consistency 10/10 while vulnerability magnitude varies ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ Qwen $+26.5$~pp), though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).
37	
38	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
39	Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\% to 31.4\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, only 0.3\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels (7/2,040); the dominant issue is not mislabeling but the 97.4\% step-level tie rate, which leaves the router with almost no per-step signal to learn from. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.
40	
41	\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}
42	Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\%, 100\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.
43	
44	\section*{Ethics Statement}
```

Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\\% to 31.4\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, only 0.3\\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels (7/2,040); the dominant issue is not mislabeling but the 97.4\\% step-level tie rate, which leaves the router with almost no per-step signal to learn from. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}\nEpisodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 35,
      "totalLines": 672
    }

114	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine computation mode differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
115	
116	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
117	\label{app:per-task}
118	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types for analysis. Types are excluded when they were used for pipeline development (5 tasks, standard hold-out practice), are taxonomically redundant with a retained family representative (7 tasks), or exhibit near-zero task progress under both strategies (7 tasks, agent capability floor).\footnote{Excluded tasks exhibit even stronger death dominance than included tasks ($\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp vs.\ $+28.7$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}).}

10	
11	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
12	\label{sec:collection}
13	
14	Per-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.
15	
16	\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}
17	For a task instance $t$ and modality $m \in \mathcal{M}$, a \emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:

          " \\label{sec:router-diagnostic}",
          " ",
          "-We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, 97.4\\% of comparisons are ties and only 0.3\\% of training steps conflict directionally with episode labels, leaving minimal step-level signal for the router to exploit. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.         "lines": [
          " Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct cross-model replication (11 task types, $N{=}220$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) shows death dominance replicates with direction consistency 10/10 while vulnerability magnitude varies ($+30.0$~pp vs.\\ Qwen $+26.5$~pp), though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}",
          "-Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\\% to 31.4\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, only 0.3\\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels (7/2,040); the dominant issue is not mislabeling but the 97.4\\% step-level tie rate, which leaves the router with almost no per-step signal to learn from. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.",
          "+Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\\% to 31.4\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label); the remaining apparent mislabeling consists of episode labels applied to steps where neither strategy has a step-level advantage (60.3\\% pre-event neutral). The issue is granularity mismatch---applying correct-direction labels to uninformative steps---not directional error. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}",
          " Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern."
        ]

    "oldString": "At step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) and only 0.3\\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels. This near-complete absence of step-level signal---rather than label noise---explains why episode-level analysis is the appropriate granularity and why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
    "newString": "At step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) and only 0.3\\% of training steps exhibit directional conflict with episode labels. This near-complete absence of step-level signal---rather than label noise---explains why episode-level analysis is the appropriate granularity and why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

        "lines": [
          " \\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}",
          " \\label{sec:router-diagnostic}",
          " ",
          "-We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label); the remaining non-tie steps are episode labels applied where neither strategy has a step-level advantage (60.3\\% pre-event neutral), a granularity mismatch rather than label error, leaving minimal signal for the router to exploit. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.",
          "+We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label) and 60.3\\% are pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged)---a granularity mismatch, not label error---leaving minimal signal for the router. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations})."
        ]
